<a id="stmt-def-norm"></a>
<div id="def:norm" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:s27"></a>
**Proposition 7** (Small change of operator norm). *For every $t\ge0$, $$\label{eq:s27}
 0\le T_0-T_t\le 2t\,T_0P^kT_0\quad\text{entrywise},\qquad
 m_t\ge m_0-2t\rho^km_0^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-def-norm"><summary>Proof of stmt-def-norm</summary>

*Proof.* Expand $-\partial_tT_t(x,y)$ as a nonnegative sum over templates and discard the factor $e^{-tN^V_{xy}}\le1$. By <a href="#def:calibration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:calibration">25</a>, the term for $w$ is at most $$\mu_u(w)\bigl(T_0(x,u)T_0(v,y)+T_0(x,v)T_0(u,y)\bigr).$$ Indeed the two exterior connections in each orientation belong to distinct clusters, so BK bounds their joint probability by the product of the corresponding full-graph connection probabilities. Add the discarded raw paths to this upper bound and sum over bases and endpoints. Each orientation gives $T_0P^kT_0$, since $P^k$ is symmetric. Integration from zero to $t$ proves the entrywise assertion. Domination by a nonnegative kernel gives $$\lVert T_0-T_t\rVert\le 2t\lVert T_0P^kT_0\rVert
                  \le2t\rho^km_0^2.$$ The triangle inequality proves the norm assertion. In particular, no positive-semidefiniteness assertion for $T_t$ is used. ◻

</details>

The norm estimate is one half of the comparison. We now state the corresponding lower bound on the rate at which the averaged row sum decreases. Let $\mathbb E_{\rm path}$ denote averaging over the type-weighted root $u=o$ and a length-$k$ $P$-path from $u$. Denote its endpoint by $v$, whether or not the path is retained as a template, and put $a_3=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T_0^3)$.

<a id="stmt-def-decrease"></a>
<div id="def:decrease" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:s31"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Decrease of the averaged susceptibility). *For all $t\ge0$, $$\label{eq:s31}
 -\chi_t'\ge e^{-tc_k}
 \left(
 \mathbb E_{\rm path}\left[
       \mathbf 1_{\{\ell_w\ge3\}}s_t^{D_w}(u)s_t^{D_w}(v)\right]
       -C'(\chi_0^+)^2\rho^ka_3
 \right),$$ where $C'>0$ depends only on $G$. The product term is defined to be zero for discarded raw paths.*

</div>

The product measures the weighted connection mass available at the two exterior endpoints. We will prove this bound by estimating the loss under deletion, separating the two exterior clusters, and comparing their penalties with that of the full connection. To make the product dominate the error, we still need paths on which both endpoint potentials remain large. Constructing such paths is the task of the next section.

<a id="stmt-def-deletion"></a>
<div id="def:deletion" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:s28"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Deletion estimate). *For $A\subset D$, let $\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[A]$ denote $A$ together with all its neighbors in the full graph. For every vertex $v$, $$\label{eq:s28}
 s_t^{D\setminus A}(v)
 \ge s_t^D(v)-\chi_0^+\sum_{z\in\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[A]}T_0(v,z).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-def-deletion"><summary>Proof of stmt-def-deletion</summary>

*Proof.* We first compare weighted connections in a fixed configuration. Suppose $W_t^D(v,y)>W_t^{D\setminus A}(v,y)$. There was an old connection. If deletion destroys it, an old simple $v$–$y$ path visits $A$; this includes the case of a deleted terminal. Otherwise some positive-weight template is newly counted after deletion. Its retained path already lay in $D$ and was open there. The raw path and its weight have not changed, so there are only two possible reasons it was not counted before.

First, a path edge may previously have been nonpivotal. An old simple connection avoiding that edge must visit $A$, since otherwise the edge would remain nonpivotal after deletion. Second, a blocker may previously have been open. Since it is now absent, it joins an internal retained-path vertex $z\notin A$ to a vertex of $A$. Every surviving simple connection uses the new template path and therefore visits this $z\in\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[A]$. This second case includes a removed pendant branch: its attachment vertex, rather than the removed vertex, lies on the connection.

The entire positive loss, which is at most one, is thus bounded by the indicator that some old simple $v$–$y$ path visits $\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[A]$. Splitting such a path at a visited vertex $z$ gives edge-disjoint connection witnesses from $v$ to $z$ and from $z$ to $y$. A union bound and BK yield $$\mathbb E_p\bigl(W_t^D(v,y)-W_t^{D\setminus A}(v,y)\bigr)
 \le\sum_{z\in\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[A]}T_0(v,z)T_0(z,y).$$ There is no sum over penalty weights in this estimate: a single visiting event bounds the whole loss. Summing over $y$ proves <a href="#eq:s28" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s28">[eq:s28]</a>. For infinite $A$ all sums are interpreted monotonically, with an infinite right-hand error giving a trivial bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-def-correlation"></a>
<div id="def:correlation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:s29"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Two distinct exterior clusters). *There is a constant $C'>0$ such that, for every induced domain $D$ and $u,v\in D$, $$\label{eq:s29}
 \mathbb E_p\bigl(X_t^D(u)X_t^D(v);\ C_u^D\ne C_v^D\bigr)
 \ge s_t^D(u)s_t^D(v)-C'(\chi_0^+)^2T_0^3(u,v).$$ The constant is independent of $D,p,k,t$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-def-correlation"><summary>Proof of stmt-def-correlation</summary>

*Proof.* Expose the exact finite cluster $A=C_u^D$, including every internal edge bit and its closed boundary. This determines $X_t^D(u)$: every counted template has retained path in $A$, its weight is deterministic, and its blockers leaving $A$ are closed or absent. The edges with both endpoints in $D\setminus A$ remain independent Bernoulli variables of parameter $p$.

<a id="def:cluster-exposure"></a>
For terminals in a cluster outside $A$, weighted connections before and after deleting $A$ agree exactly. All open paths stay in that exterior cluster; every eligible counted retained path stays there too; the only blockers removed were closed contacts with $A$. Raw excursions into $A$ affect neither eligibility nor weight. Consequently, including $v\in A$ by the zero convention, $$\label{def:cluster-exposure}
 \mathbb E_p\bigl(X_t^D(u)X_t^D(v);\ C_u^D\ne C_v^D\bigr)
 =\mathbb E_p\bigl[X_t^D(u)s_t^{D\setminus A}(v)\bigr].$$ Apply <a href="#def:deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:deletion">26</a> inside this expectation and use $X_t^D(u)\le |A|$. For adjacent $a,z$, positive association with the event that their edge is open gives $pT_0(v,z)\le T_0(v,a)$. Hence $$\sum_{z\in\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[A]}T_0(v,z)
 \le(1+d/p)\sum_{a\in A}T_0(v,a).$$ The three-arm estimate <a href="#eq:s01" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s01">[eq:s01]</a>, summed over a third terminal, gives $$\begin{split}
 \mathbb E_p\bigl(|A|\mathbf 1_{\{a\in A\}}\bigr)
 &=\sum_y\mathbb P_p(u\leftrightarrow a,\ u\leftrightarrow y\text{ in }D)\\
 &\le\sum_b T_0(u,b)T_0(b,a)\sum_y T_0(b,y)
 \le\chi_0^+T_0^2(u,a).
 \end{split}$$ The error in <a href="#def:cluster-exposure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="def:cluster-exposure">[def:cluster-exposure]</a> is therefore at most $$(1+d/p)(\chi_0^+)^2\sum_a T_0^2(u,a)T_0(a,v),$$ which proves <a href="#eq:s29" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s29">[eq:s29]</a> with $C'=1+2d/{p_c}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-def-legs"></a>
<div id="def:legs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Decomposition into two legs). *On $Q_w$, suppose $x\in C_u^{D_w}$, $y\in C_v^{D_w}$, and $C_u^{D_w}\ne C_v^{D_w}$. Then <a id="eq:s30"></a>$$\label{eq:s30}
 N^V_{xy}\le c_k+N^{D_w}_{xu}+N^{D_w}_{vy}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-def-legs"><summary>Proof of stmt-def-legs</summary>

*Proof.* The total weight of all templates whose retained paths use any edge of $\gamma_w$ is at most $\ell_wc_k/k\le c_k$, by <a href="#def:edge-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="def:edge-weight">[def:edge-weight]</a>. Consider any remaining template counted for $x\leftrightarrow y$. Its retained path cannot meet $I_w$: every open edge incident to $I_w$ is an edge of $\gamma_w$. Its connected retained path thus lies in one of the two distinct exterior endpoint clusters.

If it lies in the $u$ cluster, every one of its edges is pivotal for the exterior connection $x\leftrightarrow u$. Otherwise an exterior $x$–$u$ path avoiding that edge, followed by the corridor and an exterior $v$–$y$ path, would give a global $x$–$y$ connection avoiding it. Its blockers remain closed or absent on restriction to $D_w$. Thus it is counted, with the same weight, in $N^{D_w}_{xu}$. The $v$ cluster gives $N^{D_w}_{vy}$ in the same way. This proves the bound. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-def-legs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-def-legs</summary>

*Proof of <a href="#def:decrease" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:decrease">8</a>.* Expand the negative derivative of $\chi_t$ as the sum over the starting terminal, the other terminal, and all counted templates. Use the mass-transport identity <a href="#eq:s02" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s02">[eq:s02]</a> to move the root from the starting terminal to the template base. All terms are nonnegative, so this rearrangement is justified by Tonelli’s theorem. For each template retain only the orientation $x\in C_u^{D_w}$, $y\in C_v^{D_w}$. On $Q_w$ and this exterior event, <a href="#def:legs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:legs">28</a> implies $$W_t^V(x,y)\ge
 e^{-tc_k}W_t^{D_w}(x,u)W_t^{D_w}(v,y).$$ Independence of $Q_w$ from the exterior and <a href="#def:calibration-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="def:calibration-identity">[def:calibration-identity]</a> replace its factor $c(w)$ by $\mu_u(w)$. After summing over $x,y$, we obtain $$-\chi_t'\ge e^{-tc_k}\mathbb E_{\rm path}\left[
 \mathbf 1_{\{\ell_w\ge3\}}
 \mathbb E_p\bigl(X_t^{D_w}(u)X_t^{D_w}(v);
           C_u^{D_w}\ne C_v^{D_w}\bigr)\right].$$ Apply <a href="#def:correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:correlation">27</a>. For its nonnegative error kernel, adding discarded paths only increases the average, and $$\mathbb E_{\rm path}T_0^3(u,v)
 =[\sum_v P^k(o,v)T_0^3(v,o)]
 =\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P^kT_0^3)\le\rho^k\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T_0^3).$$ The last inequality uses the positive operator $T_0^3$ and the positive trace inequality of <a href="#pre:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:trace">6</a> for the self-adjoint operator $P^k$. It requires no commutation of $P$ with $T_0$. This proves <a href="#eq:s31" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s31">[eq:s31]</a>. ◻

</details>

The deletion error can be smaller at a vertex near a corridor endpoint than at the endpoint itself. The following comparison transfers the potential from that vertex back along a fixed path.

<a id="stmt-def-endpoint"></a>
<div id="def:endpoint" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:s32"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Comparison along a path). *If a simple path in $D$ joins $b$ to $z$ and has length $l$, then $$\label{eq:s32}
 s_t^D(b)\ge e^{-tlc_k}p^l s_t^D(z).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-def-endpoint"><summary>Proof of stmt-def-endpoint</summary>

*Proof.* Let $F$ be the event that this fixed path is open. On $F$ and on a connection from $z$ to $y$, there is also a connection from $b$ to $y$. Templates counted for the latter connection whose retained paths use an edge of the fixed path have total weight at most $lc_k$, by <a href="#def:edge-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="def:edge-weight">[def:edge-weight]</a>. For any other counted template, every retained edge is pivotal for $z\leftrightarrow y$: a $z$–$y$ bypass, concatenated with the fixed $b$–$z$ path, would otherwise give a $b$–$y$ bypass. Its blocker condition is unchanged. Thus, on $F$, $$W_t^D(b,y)\ge e^{-tlc_k}W_t^D(z,y).$$ Both $\mathbf 1_F$ and $W_t^D(z,y)$ are increasing functions of the percolation bits, by <a href="#def:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:basic">24</a>. Positive association and $\mathbb P_p(F)=p^l$ give $$\mathbb E_pW_t^D(b,y)
 \ge e^{-tlc_k}\mathbb E_p[\mathbf 1_FW_t^D(z,y)]
 \ge e^{-tlc_k}p^l\mathbb E_pW_t^D(z,y).$$ Summation over $y$ proves <a href="#eq:s32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s32">[eq:s32]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="path:section"></a>
### Paths with an escape at each endpoint

We retain the notation of the corridor deformation. In particular, ${p_c}/2\le p<{p_c}$, $T_0=T_p$, and $I_w$ is the interior of the chronological loop erasure of a raw $k$-step $P$-path $w$ from $u$ to $v$. The domain $D_w=V\setminus I_w$ depends only on this path. The next lemma finds paths for which deleting $I_w$ leaves useful potentials accessible from both endpoints. All probabilities in the lemma concern auxiliary walks, independently of the percolation configuration.

Rooted reversibility and mass transport provide the general framework for the construction; see (Aldous and Lyons 2007, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[AldousLyons2007](#ref-AldousLyons2007)]. The labeled, type-conditioned experiment below supplies the particular reversal and domination identities needed here. It makes no assertion that chronological loop erasure commutes with path reversal.

<a id="stmt-path-selection"></a>
<div id="path:selection" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:s33"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Two endpoint escapes). *For every $\tau>0$ there are constants $J\in\mathbb N$, $C_1>0$, $k_0\in\mathbb N$, and $b_*>0$, depending only on $G$ and $\tau$, with the following property. For every ${p_c}/2\le p<{p_c}$, every $k\ge k_0$, and every $R\ge1$, a set of paths of $\mathbb E_{\mathrm{path}}$-probability at least $b_*$ has loop-erased length at least three and admits, from each of $u,v$, a simple path in $D_w$ of length at most $$j=J+\left\lceil C_1\log((k+1)R)\right\rceil$$ to a vertex $z$ satisfying $$\sum_{b\in\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[I_w]}T_0(z,b)\le\frac{\tau}{R}.
 \label{eq:s33}$$ The constants $J,C_1,k_0,b_*$ are fixed before $p,k,R$ are chosen.*

</div>

We first record a smoothing bound which does not involve $\lVert T_0\rVert$. It is a quasi-transitive, off-diagonal form of the random-walk averaging estimate known as Schramm’s lemma; see (Kozma 2011, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[Kozma2011](#ref-Kozma2011)] and (Hutchcroft 2019, Proposition 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[Hutchcroft2019](#ref-Hutchcroft2019)]. The finite trace gives a short proof adapted to our symmetric lazy walk.

<a id="stmt-path-smoothing"></a>
<div id="path:smoothing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Smoothing a connection kernel). *For all vertices $a,b$ and integers $l\ge0$, <a id="eq:s34"></a>$$(P^lT_0)(a,b)\le\sqrt{c_\sigma}\,\rho^l.
 \label{eq:s34}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-path-smoothing"><summary>Proof of stmt-path-smoothing</summary>

*Proof.* Positive-semidefinite Cauchy–Schwarz for $T_0$ gives $$\lvert(P^lT_0)(a,b)\rvert^2
 \le (P^lT_0P^l)(a,a)T_0(b,b).$$ Here $T_0(b,b)=1$. The invariant positive operator $P^lT_0P^l$ has every diagonal entry bounded by $c_\sigma$ times its weighted trace. Consequently, trace cyclicity and the positive-trace bound give $$(P^lT_0P^l)(a,a)
 \le c_\sigma\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P^{2l}T_0)
 \le c_\sigma\lVert P^{2l}\rVert\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T_0)
 =c_\sigma\rho^{2l}.$$ This argument uses no commutation between $P$ and $T_0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-path-smoothing-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-path-smoothing</summary>

*Proof of <a href="#path:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:selection">30</a>.* Let $L_{\mathrm{type}}$ and $c_*>0$ be such that every transition probability of the finite type chain at every time $L\ge
L_{\mathrm{type}}$ is at least $c_*$. The same $c_*$ works for all types and will therefore remain fixed as $J$ varies.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### The labeled experiment.

For an integer $J$ to be chosen, take a geodesic segment $\gamma=(\gamma_{-J},\ldots,\gamma_J)$. Such segments exist because an infinite connected locally finite graph has infinite diameter. Write $\mathcal S=\Gamma\gamma$ for its orbit as an ordered full segment, $O_i=\Gamma\gamma_i$, and $\vartheta_i$ for the root weight of $O_i$. Different indices may describe the same vertex orbit. For $x\in O_i$, let $$N_i=\#\{g\in\mathcal S:g_i=x\}.$$ This is a positive finite number independent of the choice of $x$ in $O_i$. Indeed an anchored segment is contained in the finite ball $B(x,2J)$, and automorphisms give bijections between anchor sets. The full orbit $\mathcal S$ itself need not be finite.

Fix $u\in O_0$ and put $L=k-2J\ge L_{\mathrm{type}}$. Sample $g$ uniformly among segments with $g_0=u$. Follow its positive half from $u$ to $g_J$, then run a middle $P$-walk $\eta$ of length $L$ conditioned to end in $O_J$. Its conditioning probability $$q_L=P^L(a,O_J),\qquad a\in O_J,$$ is constant on $O_J$ and is at least $c_*$. Given $\eta$, sample $h$ uniformly among segments with $h_J=\eta_L$, and follow $(h_J,h_{J-1},\ldots,h_0)$ to $v=h_0$. The resulting raw path $w$ has length $k$.

Independently, after these choices, take ordinary $P$-walks $\alpha$ and $\beta$ of a common length $m$ from $g_{-J}$ and $h_{-J}$. Prepend the corresponding negative segment to each walk. These are the two candidate escapes, of length $J+m$; see <a href="#fig:escape-templates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:escape-templates">4</a>.

<figure id="fig:escape-templates">

<figcaption>The anchored path experiment. The blue raw path has a prescribed prefix, a reversible middle walk, and a prescribed suffix. The two orange escapes use the opposite halves of the same geodesic segments and walk tails that, conditional on $g, η, h$, are independent ordinary $P$-walks before avoidance is imposed. Reversing the middle walk and exchanging the two segments and escape tails gives the reversed raw-path law after transport on the center orbit. Avoidance is tested against the raw path; the escapes may intersect each other. Loop-erasing either escape keeps it outside the corridor interior.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-escape-templates"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:escape-templates`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\small,
  dot/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.6pt},
  raw/.style={very thick,blue!65!black,-{Stealth[length=2mm]}},
  escape/.style={thick,orange!70!black,-{Stealth[length=2mm]}}]
\node[dot,label={above left:$u=g_0$}] (u) at (0,0) {};
\node[dot,label=above:$g_J$] (a) at (1.5,1.35) {};

\draw[raw] (u)--node[above left] {$J$} (a);
\draw[raw] (a) to[out=-10,in=170] (2.6,1.1)
  to[out=-10,in=180] (4,1.3) to[out=0,in=190] (b);
\node[align=center] at (4,2.65) {middle walk of length $L$\\
conditioned to end in the type of $g_J$};
\draw[raw] (b)--node[above right] {$J$} (v);

\draw[escape] (v)--node[right] {$J$} (d);

  to[out=0,in=0] (z);
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 43 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A path configuration with a blue raw path at top and orange escape paths below: starting from $u = g_0$, a blue segment labelled $J$ goes up-right to $g_J$, connects via a horizontal dotted segment to $h_J$ labelled "middle walk of length $L$ conditioned to end in the type of $g_J$", and a blue segment labelled $J$ goes down-right to $v = h_0$. From $u = g_0$, an orange segment labelled $J$ goes down-left to $g_{-J}$ with an orange walk tail extending to $z$. From $v = h_0$, an orange segment labelled $J$ goes down-left to $h_{-J}$ with an orange walk tail extending to $z'$. Text between tails reads: "Walk tails of length $m = j - J$".
```


<a id="path:labeled-weight"></a>
Write $Q_u$ for the law of the full labeled object $\xi=(g,\eta,h,\alpha,\beta)$, and $\mu(\zeta)$ for the product of the transition probabilities along any walk $\zeta$. Its exact probability is $$Q_u(\xi)=
 \frac{\mu(\eta)\mu(\alpha)\mu(\beta)}{N_0N_Jq_L}.
 \label{path:labeled-weight}$$

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Reversal and comparison with the ordinary walk.

The labeled law serves two purposes. Reversal lets us estimate avoidance at one endpoint and transfer the estimate to the other. Domination by the ordinary walk law then converts successful labeled experiments into a positive mass of the raw paths used in the deformation.

<a id="path:rooted-reversal"></a>
There is a rooted reversal of this experiment: $$\xi^\dagger=(h,\overleftarrow\eta,g,\beta,\alpha),$$ rooted at $v$. Symmetry of $P$ shows that $Q_u(\xi)=Q_v(\xi^\dagger)$ by <a href="#path:labeled-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:labeled-weight">[path:labeled-weight]</a>; neither $N_0=N_J$ nor equality of the center and endpoint type weights is needed. The escape tails are swapped, rather than reversed. For an invariant nonnegative function $F$ of the labeled object, apply <a href="#eq:s02" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s02">[eq:s02]</a> to $$M_F(x,y)=\sum_{\xi:x\to y}Q_x(\xi)F(\xi),
 \qquad x,y\in O_0,$$ and set this transport to zero off $O_0\times O_0$. Invariance makes the outgoing sum constant on $O_0$, and the common root weight $\vartheta_0$ cancels. The reversal bijection identifies the incoming sum with the outgoing sum for $F\circ\dagger$. Thus $$\mathbb E_{Q_u}F=\mathbb E_{Q_u}(F\circ\dagger).
 \label{path:rooted-reversal}$$

<a id="path:domination"></a>
We also need domination after forgetting all labels. For a fixed raw path $w$, let $n_g$ and $n_h$ count the full segments compatible with its visible first and last blocks, respectively. Its middle is fixed, and summing all compatible full segments and all escape tails gives $$Q_u(w)=\frac{\mu(\eta)}{q_L}
        \frac{n_g}{N_0}\frac{n_h}{N_J}
 \le \frac{\mu(\eta)}{c_*}.$$ The two fractions are at most one. In particular, different negative halves which project to the same visible half cause no additional multiplicity. The ordinary walk probability is $\mu_u(w)=(2d)^{-2J}\mu(\eta)$, so $$Q_u(w)\le D_J\mu_u(w),\qquad
 D_J=\frac{(2d)^{2J}}{c_*}.
 \label{path:domination}$$

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Avoiding the raw path.

<a id="path:early-collisions"></a>
We bound all collisions uniformly in the escape-tail length $m$. First consider the escape from $u$, conditional on $g$. Its deterministic negative half and the positive half of $g$ meet only at $u$, which is allowed. Before conditioning the middle endpoint type, the probability that middle time $r$ visits any one of the $J+1$ negative-segment vertices is at most $\rho^r$, and is zero for $r<J$. Similarly, at escape-tail time $l$ the probability of visiting any one of the $J+1$ positive-segment vertices is at most $\rho^l$, and is zero for $l<J$. For a collision of the random escape tail and the middle at times $l,r$, their independence and symmetry of $P$ give $$\sum_xP^l(g_{-J},x)P^r(g_J,x)
 =P^{l+r}(g_{-J},g_J)\le\rho^{l+r}.$$ This probability is zero when $l+r<2J$. Conditioning the middle endpoint costs at most $c_*^{-1}$ in these estimates. Hence all these collisions have total probability at most $$E_J=\frac1{c_*}\left(
 2(J+1)\sum_{r\ge J}\rho^r
 +\sum_{n\ge2J}(n+1)\rho^n\right),
 \qquad E_J\longrightarrow0.
 \label{path:early-collisions}$$ Extending the time sums to infinity is what makes this bound uniform in $L$ and $m$.

For collisions with the last block, define the anchored-position kernel $$H_{a,b}(x,y)=
 \mathbf 1_{\{x\in O_a\}}
 \frac{\#\{g\in\mathcal S:g_a=x,\ g_b=y\}}{N_a}.$$ Its row sums are one on $O_a$ and zero elsewhere. Its column sum is constant on $O_b$, is zero elsewhere, and, by <a href="#eq:s02" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s02">[eq:s02]</a>, is exactly <a id="path:position-kernel"></a>$$\sum_xH_{a,b}(x,y)=\frac{\vartheta_a}{\vartheta_b}
 \quad(y\in O_b),\qquad
 \lVert H_{a,b}\rVert\le
 \sqrt{\frac{\vartheta_a}{\vartheta_b}}\le\sqrt{c_\sigma}.
 \label{path:position-kernel}$$ Thus the operator bound is independent of $J$ and of all anchor counts. Conditional on $g$, the middle endpoint distribution $\nu$ satisfies $\lVert\nu\rVert_2\le c_*^{-1}\rho^L$. Each suffix-position distribution $\nu H_{J,i}$, $0\le i\le J$, therefore has norm at most $\sqrt{c_\sigma}\,c_*^{-1}\rho^L$.

Conditional on $g$, the first escape is independent of the middle and $h$. Its deterministic positions are point masses, and the distribution at tail time $l$ has norm at most $\rho^l$. Cauchy–Schwarz and a union bound now show that its collisions with the suffix have probability at most <a id="path:suffix-collisions"></a>$$F_J\rho^L,\qquad
 F_J=C\left((J+1)^2+\frac{J+1}{1-\rho}\right),
 \label{path:suffix-collisions}$$ where $C$ depends only on $G$. The estimate includes all tail times because $\sum_{l\ge0}\rho^l=(1-\rho)^{-1}$. The same suffix-position bound at $i=0$ gives $$Q_u(\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u,v)<3)
 \le \lvert B(u,2)\rvert^{1/2}\sqrt{c_\sigma}\,c_*^{-1}\rho^L
 \le C_0\rho^L,$$ with $C_0$ independent of $J$. By <a href="#path:rooted-reversal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:rooted-reversal">[path:rooted-reversal]</a>, the second escape has exactly the same avoidance-failure bound as the first: the relevant event refers only to the raw path and its reversed raw path.

Choose $J$ so large that $2E_J\le1/8$, and then choose $L_0\ge L_{\mathrm{type}}$ so large that $(2F_J+C_0)\rho^{L_0}\le1/8$. For every $L\ge L_0$ and every $m$, with probability at least $3/4$ both candidate escapes avoid every raw path vertex other than their respective starting vertices, and $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u,v)\ge3$. The escapes need not avoid each other. This choice order is valid because no anchor-count factor occurs in <a href="#path:early-collisions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:early-collisions">[path:early-collisions]</a>.

Avoidance will keep the escape paths inside $D_w$. It remains to choose their endpoints so that the deletion error in <a href="#def:deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:deletion">26</a> is small. For this we use the smoothing bound before imposing avoidance.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Testing the potentials.

<a id="path:potential-failures"></a>
Set $\lambda=-\log\rho>0$, choose $C_1$ with $\lambda C_1>1$, and put $m=\lceil C_1\log((k+1)R)\rceil$. Conditional on $g,\eta,h$, and *before imposing any avoidance event*, the two tails are independent ordinary $P$-walks. Their terminal vertices $Z_1,Z_2$ therefore satisfy, by <a href="#path:smoothing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:smoothing">31</a> and $\lvert\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[I_w]\rvert\le(d+1)(k+1)$, $$\mathbb E\left[\sum_{b\in\mathop{\mathrm{Nb}}[I_w]}T_0(Z_i,b)\,\middle|\,g,\eta,h\right]
 \le C(k+1)\rho^m,\qquad i=1,2.$$ Here the loop-erased interior is defined whether or not its length is at least three. Markov’s inequality bounds the sum of the two potential failure probabilities by $$2C\tau^{-1}R(k+1)\rho^m
 \le 2C\tau^{-1}((k+1)R)^{1-\lambda C_1}.
 \label{path:potential-failures}$$ Increase a fixed $k_0\ge2J+L_0$ until the right-hand side is at most $1/4$ for all $k\ge k_0$ and $R\ge1$. All constants remain independent of $p$ and $R$.

Subtracting the failure probability in <a href="#path:potential-failures" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:potential-failures">[path:potential-failures]</a> from the preceding $3/4$ success bound leaves probability at least $1/2$ for both avoidance and both potential tests simultaneously. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u,v)\ge3$, the retained corridor has length at least three. Its interior is contained in the raw path and excludes $u,v$. Each escape therefore lies in $D_w$, and erasing its loops gives a simple path to the same terminal vertex of length at most $J+m$. No reversal property of chronological loop erasure is used.

Project this successful event onto raw paths admitting these witnesses. By <a href="#path:domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="path:domination">[path:domination]</a>, ordinary walk law from a root in $O_0$ assigns these paths probability at least $1/(2D_J)$. Averaging over root types proves the lemma with $b_*=\vartheta_0/(2D_J)>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="con:section"></a>
### Critical boundedness and the nonuniqueness interval

We combine the preceding estimates to prove the critical operator bound. The argument fixes all path constants before choosing the subcritical parameter and the deformation time. We then transfer the bound to an interval above criticality and prove the simultaneous cluster assertion.

<details id="proof-stmt-path-smoothing-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-path-smoothing</summary>

<a id="eq:s35"></a>
*Proof of <a href="#thm:critical-operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:critical-operator">2</a>.* Suppose that the critical connection kernel $T_{{p_c}}$ is unbounded. Put $p_0={p_c}/2$. For $p_0\le p<{p_c}$, use the corridor notation $$\delta={p_c}-p,\qquad S=\log(1/\delta),\qquad
 m_0=\lVert T_p\rVert,\qquad R=\frac{\chi_0^+}{m_0}\ge1,
 \qquad a_3=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T_p^3).$$ Sprinkling <a href="#eq:s05" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s05">[eq:s05]</a> with $a=\delta/(1-p)$ would bound $T_{{p_c}}$ if $ad m_0<1$. The assumed unboundedness, together with <a href="#eq:s22" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s22">[eq:s22]</a> and <a href="#eq:s23" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s23">[eq:s23]</a>, therefore gives $$m_0\ge\frac{1-p}{d\delta},\qquad
 \log R=o(S),\qquad \log a_3=o(S)
 \quad(\delta\downarrow0).
 \label{eq:s35}$$ Indeed $m_0\ge c\delta^{-1}$, whereas, for every $\eta>0$, $\chi_0^+\le C_\eta\delta^{-1-\eta}$ and $1\le a_3\le C_\eta\delta^{-\eta}$. Hence $1\le R\le C'_\eta\delta^{-\eta}$ for every $\eta>0$.

<a id="stmt-con-row-comparison"></a>
<div id="con:row-comparison" class="claim">

<a id="eq:s36"></a>
**Claim 1** (Comparison of deformed row sums). *There is $c_f>0$, depending only on $G$, such that $$s_t^V(z)\ge c_f\chi_t
 \qquad\text{for every $z\in V$ whenever $tc_k\le1$}.
 \label{eq:s36}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-row-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-row-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Connectedness and the finite number of vertex types give an integer $L_f$ such that every vertex is within distance $L_f$ of every vertex orbit. For fixed $t$, choose an orbit on which $s_t^V$ is maximal and a simple path of length at most $L_f$ from $z$ to that orbit. Equation <a href="#eq:s32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s32">[eq:s32]</a>, with $p\ge p_0$, gives $$s_t^V(z)\ge e^{-L_f}p_0^{L_f}\max_x s_t^V(x)
 \ge e^{-L_f}p_0^{L_f}\chi_t.$$ Thus $c_f=e^{-L_f}p_0^{L_f}$ is admissible. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:s37"></a>
Fix $0<\tau<c_f/(4c_\sigma)$ and the constants of <a href="#path:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:selection">30</a> for this value of $\tau$. They are now fixed throughout the limit $p\uparrow{p_c}$. Since $j\le J+1+C_1\log((k+1)R)$, there are fixed constants $c>0$ and $C_2>0$ such that $$p^{2j}\ge c(kR)^{-C_2}\qquad(k\ge k_0,\ R\ge1).
 \label{eq:s37}$$ For example, one may take $C_2=2C_1\log(1/p_0)$ and $c=p_0^{2(J+1)}2^{-C_2}$.

<a id="eq:s38"></a>
Put $\lambda=-\log\rho>0$ and choose a fixed $K_*$ with $\kappa:=\lambda K_*>C_2+4$. Set $$A_\delta=\log(2R)+\log(2+a_3)+\log S,\qquad
 k=\lceil K_*A_\delta\rceil,\qquad
 B_*=(kR)^{C_2+2},\qquad t_*=\frac{B_*}{m_0}.$$ By <a href="#eq:s35" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s35">[eq:s35]</a>, $k\to\infty$ and $k=o(S)$; in particular $k\ge k_0$ and $k\le S$ eventually. The estimates needed below are $$B_*\rho^k=o(1),\qquad
 R^2a_3\rho^k=o((kR)^{-C_2}),\qquad
 t_*jc_k=o(1).
 \label{eq:s38}$$ To check their simultaneous validity, the definition of $k$ gives $$\rho^k\le (2R)^{-\kappa}(2+a_3)^{-\kappa}S^{-\kappa}.$$ Since $R,a_3\ge1$ and $k\le S$, it follows that $$B_*\rho^k\le C S^{C_2+2-\kappa}\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 (kR)^{C_2}R^2a_3\rho^k
 \le C S^{C_2-\kappa}\longrightarrow0.$$ Finally $c_k=e^{Ck}$ and $m_0\ge c e^S$, so $$\log(t_*jc_k)
 \le -S+(C_2+2)(\log k+\log R)+\log j+Ck+O(1)
 =-S+o(S).$$ This proves <a href="#eq:s38" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s38">[eq:s38]</a> with every path constant already fixed.

<a id="con:retained-norm"></a>
Consider the deformation with these values of $p,k$, on $0\le t\le t_*$. Equation <a href="#eq:s27" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s27">[eq:s27]</a> and the first estimate in <a href="#eq:s38" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s38">[eq:s38]</a> give $$m_t\ge m_0(1-2B_*\rho^k)\ge\frac{m_0}{2},\qquad
 \chi_t\ge\frac{m_0}{2c_\sigma}.
 \label{con:retained-norm}$$ The second inequality uses the row-sum bound $m_t\le c_\sigma\chi_t$; it requires no positivity of $T_t$ as an operator.

For a selected raw path in <a href="#path:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:selection">30</a>, let $z$ be either of its two escape endpoints. By <a href="#eq:s28" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s28">[eq:s28]</a>, <a href="#eq:s33" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s33">[eq:s33]</a>, <a href="#eq:s36" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s36">[eq:s36]</a>, and <a href="#con:retained-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:retained-norm">[con:retained-norm]</a>, $$\begin{split}
 s_t^{D_w}(z)
 &\ge c_f\chi_t-\chi_0^+\frac{\tau}{R}
  =c_f\chi_t-\tau m_0\\
 &\ge(c_f-2c_\sigma\tau)\chi_t
  \ge\frac{c_f}{2}\chi_t.
 \end{split}$$ The hypotheses of <a href="#eq:s36" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s36">[eq:s36]</a> hold throughout this interval by the last estimate in <a href="#eq:s38" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s38">[eq:s38]</a>. Apply <a href="#eq:s32" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s32">[eq:s32]</a> to the two simple escape paths in $D_w$, each of length at most $j$. Their product is bounded below by $$s_t^{D_w}(u)s_t^{D_w}(v)
 \ge e^{-2tjc_k}p^{2j}\left(\frac{c_f}{2}\chi_t\right)^2.$$ Such paths have $\mathbb E_{\mathrm{path}}$-probability at least $b_*$, so <a href="#eq:s37" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s37">[eq:s37]</a> and <a href="#eq:s38" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s38">[eq:s38]</a> imply <a id="con:positive-term"></a>$$\mathbb E_{\mathrm{path}}\!\left[
  \mathbf 1_{\{\ell_w\ge3\}}s_t^{D_w}(u)s_t^{D_w}(v)\right]
 \ge c_1(kR)^{-C_2}\chi_t^2,
 \label{con:positive-term}$$ uniformly on $[0,t_*]$, with $c_1>0$ independent of sufficiently small $\delta$. On the other hand, the error in <a href="#eq:s31" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s31">[eq:s31]</a> is at most $$C'(\chi_0^+)^2\rho^ka_3
 =C'R^2m_0^2\rho^ka_3
 \le4C'c_\sigma^2R^2\rho^ka_3\chi_t^2
 =o((kR)^{-C_2}\chi_t^2).$$ The prefactor $e^{-tc_k}$ in that equation is uniformly bounded below. We conclude that, for a constant $c_2>0$, <a id="con:differential-decrease"></a>$$-\chi_t'\ge c_2(kR)^{-C_2}\chi_t^2
 \qquad(0\le t\le t_*).
 \label{con:differential-decrease}$$ The differentiability and domination established for the deformation justify integration. Since $\chi_t>0$, it yields $$\frac1{\chi_{t_*}}
 \ge c_2(kR)^{-C_2}t_*
 =\frac{c_2(kR)^2}{m_0}.$$ But <a href="#con:retained-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="con:retained-norm">[con:retained-norm]</a> gives $1/\chi_{t_*}\le2c_\sigma/m_0$. These inequalities are incompatible with $kR\to\infty$. Thus $T_{{p_c}}$ is bounded, proving <a href="#thm:critical-operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:critical-operator">2</a>. ◻

</details>

The next elementary endpoint lemma records the equivalence between critical boundedness and the uniform subcritical formulation in <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="stmt-con-critical-limit"></a>
<div id="con:critical-limit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Monotone passage to an endpoint). *Let $G$ be an infinite locally finite graph, let $p_*\in(0,1]$, and let $T_p(x,y)=\mathbb P_p(x\leftrightarrow y)$ be its Bernoulli bond connection kernels. Then $T_{p_*}$ is bounded on counting-measure $\ell^2(V)$ if and only if $\sup_{0\le p<p_*}\lVert T_p\rVert_{2\to2}<\infty$. In that case the supremum equals $\lVert T_{p_*}\rVert_{2\to2}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-critical-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-critical-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Each finite-domain connection probability is continuous in $p$, and connectivity in $G$ is the increasing union of finite-domain connectivity events. Thus $T_p(x,y)\uparrow T_{p_*}(x,y)$ as $p\uparrow p_*$. If the subcritical norms are bounded by $M$, then for finitely supported $f,g$ the finite sum $\langle f,T_pg\rangle$ tends to $\langle f,T_{p_*}g\rangle$ and has absolute value at most $M\lVert f\rVert_2\lVert g\rVert_2$. The limiting sesquilinear form extends by density to a bounded operator with the required matrix entries and norm at most $M$. Conversely, entrywise nonnegativity gives $$|\langle f,T_pg\rangle|\le\langle|f|,T_p|g|\rangle
 \le\langle|f|,T_{p_*}|g|\rangle
 \le\lVert T_{p_*}\rVert\lVert f\rVert_2\lVert g\rVert_2.$$ The two inequalities prove both equivalence and equality of norms. ◻

</details>

The simultaneous phase theorem of Häggström, Peres, and Schonmann (Häggström, Peres, and Schonmann 1999, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[HaggstromPeresSchonmann1999](#ref-HaggstromPeresSchonmann1999)] applies to all quasi-transitive graphs and gives one event valid throughout the open nonuniqueness phase. The following direct argument proves the compact interval consequence from operator boundedness alone. It avoids taking an uncountable intersection of events of probability one.

<a id="stmt-con-coupled-interval"></a>
<div id="con:coupled-interval" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (One coupling for an interval). *Let $G$ be infinite, connected, locally finite, and quasi-transitive. Suppose ${p_c}<p_1<p_2<1$ and $T_{p_2}$ is bounded on $\ell^2(V)$. In the coupling obtained by assigning independent uniform labels to edges and opening an edge at parameter $p$ when its label is at most $p$, almost surely there are infinitely many infinite clusters simultaneously at every $p\in[p_1,p_2]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-coupled-interval"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-coupled-interval</summary>

*Proof.* Let $N_{12}$ be the number of $p_2$-clusters containing an infinite $p_1$-cluster. First, every automorphism-invariant event of the label field has probability zero or one. Indeed, approximate such an event $E$, within error $\varepsilon$ in probability, by a finite-coordinate event $A$. Its coordinates lie in a finite ball centered at a vertex in an unbounded orbit; such an orbit exists because there are finitely many orbits and infinitely many vertices. An automorphism can move this ball disjointly from itself. The events $A$ and its translate are then independent, whereas $E$ agrees with its translate. It follows that $\lvert\mathbb P(E)-\mathbb P(E)^2\rvert\le4\varepsilon$, and letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves the assertion. In particular the invariant, measurable random variable $N_{12}\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,\infty\}$ is constant almost surely. Since $p_1>{p_c}$, an infinite $p_1$-cluster exists with positive probability, so this constant is positive.

It cannot equal one. Write $\theta(x)=\mathbb P_{p_1}(|C_x|=\infty)$. Some $\theta(x)$ is positive; positive association with a finite path makes $\theta$ positive at every vertex. Invariance and the finite number of types give $\theta_*:=\inf_x\theta(x)>0$. If $N_{12}=1$, any two vertices in infinite $p_1$-clusters belong to the same $p_2$-cluster. Positive association therefore implies $$T_{p_2}(x,y)\ge\theta(x)\theta(y)\ge\theta_*^2
 \qquad(x,y\in V).$$ For finite sets $F$ of arbitrarily large size this gives $\langle\mathbf 1_F,T_{p_2}\mathbf 1_F\rangle/\lVert\mathbf 1_F\rVert_2^2
\ge\theta_*^2|F|$, contradicting boundedness.

Suppose instead that $N_{12}=n$ almost surely for an integer $n\ge2$. By countability there are fixed vertices $x,y$ which, with positive probability, belong to infinite $p_1$-clusters lying in distinct $p_2$-clusters. Fix a finite path from $x$ to $y$. Replace its edge labels by independent uniform labels in $[0,p_1]$. The resulting law is absolutely continuous with respect to the original product law: only finitely many coordinates were resampled, each with a bounded density. At both parameters this modification only opens edges, and on the specified positive-probability event it merges two counted $p_2$-clusters.

Every infinite $p_1$-cluster after the modification contains an infinite $p_1$-cluster from before it. Indeed finitely many added edges can combine only finitely many old clusters into any new cluster; a finite union of finite clusters is finite. Thus each new counted $p_2$-cluster contains an old counted $p_2$-cluster, and old $p_2$-clusters only merge. On the event in question the new count is at most $n-1$. This contradicts absolute continuity and the almost sure equality $N_{12}=n$. Hence $N_{12}=\infty$ almost surely.

On this single event, choose an infinite $p_1$-cluster in each of infinitely many distinct counted $p_2$-clusters. For every $p\in[p_1,p_2]$, their containing $p$-clusters are infinite and remain distinct because their $p_2$-clusters are distinct. This proves the simultaneous assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-con-coupled-interval-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-coupled-interval</summary>

*Proof of <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* First suppose $G$ is simple. If $\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}(G)$ is unimodular, <a href="#thm:critical-operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:critical-operator">2</a> gives $\lVert T_{{p_c}}\rVert<\infty$. Since ${p_c}<1$, sprinkling <a href="#eq:s05" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:s05">[eq:s05]</a> at $p={p_c}$ with any sufficiently small $a>0$ gives a bounded connection kernel at ${p_c}+(1-{p_c})a>{p_c}$. Thus ${p_c}<{p_{2\to 2}}$.

If $\mathop{\mathrm{Aut}}(G)$ is nonunimodular, <a href="#pre:unimodular-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:unimodular-reduction">1</a> supplies both critical boundedness and the strict operator gap. In either case, <a href="#pre:threshold-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:threshold-order">4</a> yields ${p_c}<{p_{2\to 2}}\le{p_u}$.

If loops or parallel edges are allowed, use the simple subdivision $H$ from <a href="#pre:simple-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:simple-reduction">1</a>. Infinite-cluster counts in $H$ at parameter $t$ agree with those in $G$ at parameter $t^2$, so ${p_c}(H)^2={p_c}(G)$. Moreover, on the old vertex set, $$T^H_t\big|_{V(G)\times V(G)}=T^G_{t^2}.$$ Compression of a bounded operator to this coordinate subspace is bounded. Applying the simple-graph result to $H$ therefore gives boundedness of $T^G_{{p_c}(G)}$ and of $T^G_p$ at some $p>{p_c}(G)$. The inequality ${p_{2\to 2}}(G)\le{p_u}(G)$ again follows from <a href="#pre:threshold-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:threshold-order">4</a>. This covers the full graph class in the statement.

Finally choose ${p_c}<p_1<p_2<1$ with $T_{p_2}$ bounded, which the strict operator gap permits. [con:coupled-interval](#con:coupled-interval) supplies the claimed simultaneous nonempty interval of infinitely many infinite clusters. The equality of the uniform subcritical and critical norms is <a href="#con:critical-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="con:critical-limit">32</a>, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="cons:section"></a>
### Consequences of the operator theorem

We now prove Corollary <a href="#cor:critical-laws" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:critical-laws">2</a>. The two parts of the connection-operator conclusion play related but distinct roles. Boundedness at criticality makes the triangle diagram finite, bringing the standard mean-field theory into play. The strict interval of operator boundedness above ${p_c}$ gives exponential two-point decay there and supplies the stronger input used for the extrinsic-radius law.

<details id="proof-stmt-con-coupled-interval-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-con-coupled-interval</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:critical-laws" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:critical-laws">2</a>.* For every graph in the corollary, <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> makes $T_{{p_c}}$ a bounded operator on counting-measure $\ell^2(V)$. Tonelli’s theorem identifies its nonnegative matrix product with the triangle sum. If $\delta_v$ is the unit vector at $v$, then $$\nabla_{{p_c}}(v)
 =\langle\delta_v,T_{{p_c}}^{3}\delta_v\rangle
 \le\lVert T_{{p_c}}^{3}\rVert_{2\to2}
 \le\lVert T_{{p_c}}\rVert_{2\to2}^{3}.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:critical-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:critical-triangle">[eq:critical-triangle]</a>. The same implication is recorded in (Hutchcroft 2020d, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[Hutchcroft2020L2](#ref-Hutchcroft2020L2)].

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Critical laws on simple graphs.

First suppose that $G$ is simple. The susceptibility, percolation-probability, and cluster-size estimates are the classical mean-field consequences of the triangle condition (Aizenman and Newman 1984; Barsky and Aizenman 1991) <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[AizenmanNewman1984](#ref-AizenmanNewman1984), [BarskyAizenman1991](#ref-BarskyAizenman1991)]; their quasi-transitive form is recorded in (Hutchcroft 2020d, sec. 1, Equations (1.1)–(1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[Hutchcroft2020L2](#ref-Hutchcroft2020L2)]. For explicit applications with the precise quasi-transitive hypotheses, we use the following later results. Under the stronger condition ${p_c}<{p_{2\to 2}}$ available here, we may apply (Hutchcroft 2022b, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[Hutchcroft2022FiniteClusters](#ref-Hutchcroft2022FiniteClusters)]. The corollary with $k=1$ and $p\uparrow{p_c}$ gives <a href="#eq:meanfield-chi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:meanfield-chi">[eq:meanfield-chi]</a>, since subcritical clusters are finite. The theorem at $p={p_c}$ gives <a href="#eq:meanfield-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:meanfield-size">[eq:meanfield-size]</a>, since Lemma <a href="#pre:critical-finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:critical-finiteness">2</a> excludes infinite critical clusters.

For percolation probability, Lemma 2.1 of (Hutchcroft 2022b) <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[Hutchcroft2022FiniteClusters](#ref-Hutchcroft2022FiniteClusters)], whose hypothesis is the triangle condition, bounds the intrinsic-radius tail uniformly over vertices. Letting the radius tend to infinity gives $\theta_v(p)\le C(p-{p_c})$ for $p\ge{p_c}$: local finiteness makes an infinite cluster equivalent to an unbounded intrinsic radius. For the lower bound, choose one representative from each vertex orbit, forming a finite set $F$. Theorem 2 and Propositions 11 and 23 of (Antunović and Veselić 2008) <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[AntunovicVeselic2008](#ref-AntunovicVeselic2008)], with $\beta=-\log(1-p)$, give $$\sum_{u\in F}\theta_u(p)\ge c(p-{p_c})$$ for $p>{p_c}$ sufficiently close to ${p_c}$. Indeed, their bound is linear in $\beta-\beta_c$, and this is comparable to $p-{p_c}$ near $0<{p_c}<1$. Fix $v$ and choose a path of length $l_u$ from $v$ to each $u\in F$. Harris association applied to the event that this path is open and the event $\{|C_u|=\infty\}$ gives $\theta_v(p)\ge p^{l_u}\theta_u(p)$. Hence $$\theta_v(p)\ge
 \frac{\min_{u\in F}{p_c}^{l_u}}{|F|}
 \sum_{u\in F}\theta_u(p)
 \ge c_v(p-{p_c}),$$ which proves <a href="#eq:meanfield-theta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:meanfield-theta">[eq:meanfield-theta]</a>.

For the radius estimates, it is important to distinguish the two metrics. The intrinsic-radius upper bound under the triangle condition originates in the lattice setting in (Kozma and Nachmias 2009, Theorem 1.2(ii)) <a id="pm-cite-73"></a>[[KozmaNachmias2009](#ref-KozmaNachmias2009)]; its quasi-transitive form and the matching general lower bound are described in (Hutchcroft 2020d, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[Hutchcroft2020L2](#ref-Hutchcroft2020L2)]. The extrinsic-radius law uses the stronger condition ${p_c}<{p_{2\to 2}}$ (Hutchcroft 2020d, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[Hutchcroft2020L2](#ref-Hutchcroft2020L2)]. Both estimates in the exact quasi-transitive form needed here are included in (Hutchcroft 2022b, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[Hutchcroft2022FiniteClusters](#ref-Hutchcroft2022FiniteClusters)], whose hypothesis is ${p_c}<{p_{2\to 2}}$. Set $p={p_c}$ in that theorem. Its events require the cluster radius to be finite; Lemma <a href="#pre:critical-finiteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:critical-finiteness">2</a> shows that all critical clusters are finite almost surely, so this restriction may be removed. The resulting estimates are <a href="#eq:meanfield-intrinsic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:meanfield-intrinsic">[eq:meanfield-intrinsic]</a> and <a href="#eq:meanfield-extrinsic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:meanfield-extrinsic">[eq:meanfield-extrinsic]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### Exponential connectivity.

The definition of ${p_{\exp}}$ above is equivalent to that in (Hutchcroft 2020d, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[Hutchcroft2020L2](#ref-Hutchcroft2020L2)]: a positive exponential rate is equivalent to an estimate with some positive exponent and finite prefactor, uniformly over vertices. Theorem 2.2 of that paper gives ${p_{2\to 2}}\le{p_{\exp}}$. More precisely, its Remark 2.3 gives, for $0<p<{p_{2\to 2}}$, $$T_p(x,y)\le
 2\lVert T_p\rVert_{2\to2}
 \exp\left(-\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_G(x,y)}
                 {e\lVert T_p\rVert_{2\to2}}\right)
 \qquad(x,y\in V).$$ At $p=0$ the off-diagonal connection probabilities vanish. Together with ${p_c}<{p_{2\to 2}}$ from <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, this proves <a href="#eq:exponential-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exponential-threshold">[eq:exponential-threshold]</a> and <a href="#eq:exponential-connectivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exponential-connectivity">[eq:exponential-connectivity]</a> for simple graphs.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### Subdivision and transfer of the laws.

It remains to pass from simple graphs to the full graph class of the corollary. Delete the loops, which change none of the observables, and let $H=\widetilde G$ be the simple subdivided graph in Lemma <a href="#pre:simple-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:simple-reduction">1</a>. Denote the set of nonloop edge copies of $G$ by $E_0$, so $V(H)=V(G)\sqcup E_0$. Let $d_0$ bound the number of incident nonloop edges at a vertex of $G$, counting parallel copies. In the coupling of that lemma, parameter $t$ on $H$ induces parameter $p=t^2$ on $G$, old-vertex connection probabilities agree, and ${p_c}(H)^2={p_c}(G)$.

For an old root $v$, write $C_v^G$ and $C_v^H$ for the two coupled clusters, and use the same superscripts for the connection and percolation observables. Their old vertices agree. Every new vertex in $C_v^H$ is joined by an open edge to an old neighbor in the same cluster, and every old vertex has at most $d_0$ new neighbors. Hence, pathwise, <a id="eq:subdivision-size"></a>$$\label{eq:subdivision-size}
 |C_v^G|\le |C_v^H|\le(1+d_0)|C_v^G|,
 \qquad
 \theta_v^G(t^2)=\theta_v^H(t).$$ For two old vertices, both the ambient distance and the distance along open paths in $H$ are twice their counterparts in $G$. A new vertex in the root cluster is at distance at most one from an old vertex in that cluster. Consequently, for either radius, <a id="eq:subdivision-radius"></a>$$\label{eq:subdivision-radius}
 2\operatorname{rad}_{*}(C_v^G)
 \le \operatorname{rad}_{*}(C_v^H)
 \le2\operatorname{rad}_{*}(C_v^G)+1,
 \qquad *\in\{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{ext}\}.$$ The statements include infinite values. Applied at $t={p_c}(H)=\sqrt{{p_c}(G)}$, these comparisons transfer the three critical tails from $H$ to $G$. They also transfer susceptibility and percolation probability on the appropriate sides of criticality: if $t_c=\sqrt{{p_c}(G)}>0$, then $p-{p_c}(G)=(t-t_c)(t+t_c)$, whose second factor stays bounded above and away from zero near $t_c$. Multiplicative constants may change, as allowed in the corollary.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### Transfer of the operator threshold.

To transfer the full range in <a href="#eq:exponential-connectivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exponential-connectivity">[eq:exponential-connectivity]</a>, we also compare the two operator thresholds. Define the incidence operator $B:\ell^2(E_0)\to\ell^2(V(G))$ by $B(x,e)=\mathbf 1_{\{x\text{ is an endpoint of }e\}}$. Its row sums are at most $d_0$ and its column sums are two. Cauchy–Schwarz at each old vertex gives $\lVert Bf\rVert_2^2\le2d_0\lVert f\rVert_2^2$, so $\lVert B\rVert_{2\to2}\le\sqrt{2d_0}$. The operator $$Q:\ell^2(V(G))\longrightarrow
       \ell^2(V(G))\oplus\ell^2(E_0),\qquad
 Qf=(f,B^*f),$$ satisfies $\lVert Q\rVert_{2\to2}^{2}\le1+2d_0$. For an old-to-new connection in $H$, a simple open path must enter the new vertex through one of its two old endpoints. For a connection between distinct new vertices, choose an old endpoint at each end of a simple open path. Between these old endpoints the path induces a connection in $G$. The union bound, together with equality for old-to-old connections, therefore gives the entrywise inequality $$T_t^H\le Q T_{t^2}^G Q^*.$$ The product on the right is interpreted entrywise here; each entry is a sum over at most four pairs of old endpoints. This inequality also covers a new diagonal entry: the corresponding diagonal entry of $B^*T_{t^2}^G B$ is at least two. If $T_{t^2}^G$ is bounded, entrywise domination of nonnegative kernels makes $T_t^H$ bounded with norm at most $\lVert Q\rVert^2\lVert T_{t^2}^G\rVert$. Conversely, if $T_t^H$ is bounded, compression to the old vertices makes $T_{t^2}^G$ bounded. Thus boundedness is equivalent and, in that case, $$\lVert T_{t^2}^G\rVert_{2\to2}
 \le\lVert T_t^H\rVert_{2\to2}
 \le(1+2d_0)\lVert T_{t^2}^G\rVert_{2\to2}.$$ It follows that $${p_{2\to 2}}(H)^2={p_{2\to 2}}(G).$$ For every $0\le p<{p_{2\to 2}}(G)$, apply the simple-graph exponential bound to $H$ at $t=\sqrt p<{p_{2\to 2}}(H)$. Old-vertex connectivity agrees and $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_H(x,y)=2\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_G(x,y)$ for $x,y\in V(G)$, so restriction gives <a href="#eq:exponential-connectivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exponential-connectivity">[eq:exponential-connectivity]</a> with constants depending only on $G$ and $p$. Its validity for every $p<{p_{2\to 2}}(G)$ implies ${p_{2\to 2}}(G)\le{p_{\exp}}(G)$, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

```json
      "caption": "The chain produced by the switching procedure. Cutting the selected seams recovers the inserted pieces and the initial cluster $A$. The drawing shows vertex sets and selected open seams, with internal edges and other incident edges omitted.",
      "figure_id": "fig:splicing",
      "pdf_page": 22,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:splicing",
          "exact_quote": "The chain produced by the switching procedure. Cutting the\nselected seams recovers the inserted pieces and the initial cluster\n$A$. The drawing shows vertex sets and selected open seams, with\ninternal edges and other incident edges omitted.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,\n  piece/.style={draw,rounded corners=8pt,fill=blue!5,minimum width=1.65cm,minimum height=1.05cm},\n  seam/.style={very thick,blue!65!black},dot/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.4pt}]\n\\node[piece,minimum width=2cm] (a) at (0.3,0) {$A$};\n\\node[piece] (b1) at (3.1,0) {$B_{i_1}$};\n\\node[piece] (b2) at (5.8,0) {$B_{i_2}$};\n\\node[piece] (bt) at (8.5,0) {$B_{i_t}$};\n\\draw[seam] (a.east)--node[above] {$e_{i_1}$} (b1.west);\n\\draw[seam] (b1.east)--node[above] {$e_{i_2}$} (b2.west);\n\\draw[seam,densely dotted] (b2.east)--node[above] {$\\cdots$} (bt.west);\n\\node[dot,label=below:$x$] at (-0.2,0.10) {};\n\\node[dot,label=below:$z$] at (8.9,0.10) {};\n\\node[align=center,text width=10.7cm] at (4.3,-1.25)\n{Each selected piece is attached by one open seam.\\\\\nThe seams occur in order among the bridges separating $x$ and $z$.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal chain of connected components: an oval set $A$ containing point $x$ on the left, connected via open seam edge $e_{i_1}$ to a rounded rectangular set $B_{i_1}$, connected via open seam edge $e_{i_2}$ to set $B_{i_2}$, followed by continuation dots $\\cdots$ leading to set $B_{i_t}$ containing point $z$. Text below the shapes reads: \"Each selected piece is attached by one open seam.\" and \"The seams occur in order among the bridges separating $x$ and $z$.\""
    },
    {
      "caption": "Two hanging components in a common switched root cluster. Undoing both insertions leaves $A_{00}$. Graph nonadjacency makes the two collections of exchange masks disjoint.           "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,\n  piece/.style={draw,rounded corners=8pt,fill=blue!5,minimum width=1.65cm,minimum height=1.05cm},\n  seam/.style={very thick,blue!65!black},dot/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.4pt}]\n\\node[piece,minimum width=2.2cm] (root) at (0.4,0) {$A_{00}$};\n\\node[piece,minimum width=2.1cm] (upper) at (4.3,.35) {$B^1$};\n\\node[piece,minimum width=2.1cm] (lower) at (4.3,-1.1) {$B^2$};\n\\draw[seam] (root.east) to[bend left=10] node[above] {first seam} (upper.west);\n\\draw[seam] (root.south east) to[bend right=8] node[below,sloped] {first seam} (lower.west);\n\\node[dot,label=below:$x$] at (-0.2,-.05) {};\n\\node[dot,label=right:$z_1$] at (4.75,.35) {};\n\\node[dot,label=right:$z_2$] at (4.75,-1.1) {};\n\\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (5.7,.87)--(5.7,-1.62);\n\\node[align=left,anchor=west,text width=3cm] at (6,-.35)\n{No underlying graph edge joins $B^1$ to $B^2$.       "witness_description": "An oval set $A_{00}$ containing point $x$ on the left, with two edges labelled \"first seam\" emerging from it: one connecting upwards to a rounded rectangular set $B^1$ containing point $z_1$, and one connecting downwards to a rounded rectangular set $B^2$ containing point $z_2$. Text to the right states: \"No underlying graph edge joins $B^1$ to $B^2$. Their exchange masks are disjoint.\""

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,\n  dot/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.8pt},\n  open/.style={very thick,blue!65!black},\n  closed/.style={densely dashed,red!65!black}]\n\\fill[blue!5,rounded corners=12pt] (-1.9,-.95) rectangle (.3,.95);\n\\draw[blue!30!black,rounded corners=12pt] (-1.9,-.95) rectangle (.3,.95);\n\\fill[blue!5,rounded corners=12pt] (7.7,-.95) rectangle (9.9,.95);\n\\draw[blue!30!black,rounded corners=12pt] (7.7,-.95) rectangle (9.9,.95);\n\\node at (-1,.55) {$C_u^{D_w}$};\n\\node at (8.9,.55) {$C_v^{D_w}$};\n\\node[dot,label=below:$x$] (x) at (-1.35,-.35) {};\n\\node[dot,label=above:$u$] (u) at (0,0) {};\n\\node[dot,label=above:$v$] (v) at (8,0) {};\n\\node[dot,label=below:$y$] (y) at (9.2,-.35) {};\n\\draw[open] (x)--(-.8,-.12)--(u);\n\\draw[open] (v)--(8.6,-.12)--(y);\n\\foreach \\q in {1.6,3.2,4.8,6.4}{\n  \\node[dot] at (\\q,0) {};\n  \\draw[closed] (\\q,0)--(\\q+.25,.7);\n  \\draw[closed] (\\q,0)--(\\q-.25,-.7);\n  \\draw[red!65!black] (\\q+.055,.37)--(\\q+.195,.29);\n  \\draw[red!65!black] (\\q-.195,-.29)--(\\q-.055,-.37);\n}\n\\draw[open] (u)--(v);\n\\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (1.3,1.1)--(6.7,1.1)\n  node[midway,above=5pt] {interior $I_w$};\n\\node[align=center,text width=10.5cm] at (4,-1.5)\n{The corridor edges are open; all other edges incident to $I_w$ are closed.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal corridor between sets $C_u^{D_w}$ on the left (containing point $x$ and endpoint $u$) and $C_v^{D_w}$ on the right (containing point $y$ and endpoint $v$). The corridor is labelled \"interior $I_w$\" above, consisting of open horizontal edges linking vertices between $u$ and $v$, with dashed stubs extending upward and downward from each internal vertex. Text below reads: \"The corridor edges are open; all other edges incident to $I_w$ are closed.\""
    },
    {
      "caption": "The anchored path experiment. The blue raw path has a prescribed prefix, a reversible middle walk, and a prescribed suffix. The two orange escapes use the opposite halves of the same geodesic segments and walk tails that, conditional on $g, η, h$, are independent ordinary $P$-walks before avoidance is imposed. Reversing the middle walk and exchanging the two segments and escape tails gives the reversed raw-path law after transport on the center orbit. Avoidance is tested against the raw path; the escapes may intersect each other. Loop-erasing either escape keeps it outside the corridor interior.",
      "figure_id": "fig:escape-templates",
      "pdf_page": 43,
      "source_evidence": [
        {

          "source_object": "fig:0004"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,\n  dot/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.6pt},\n  raw/.style={very thick,blue!65!black,-{Stealth[length=2mm]}},\n  escape/.style={thick,orange!70!black,-{Stealth[length=2mm]}}]\n\\node[dot,label={above left:$u=g_0$}] (u) at (0,0) {};\n\\node[dot,label=above:$g_J$] (a) at (1.5,1.35) {};\n\\node[dot,label=above:$h_J$] (b) at (6.5,1.35) {};\n\\node[dot,label={above right:$v=h_0$}] (v) at (8,0) {};\n\\node[dot,label=left:$g_{-J}$] (c) at (-1.1,-1.1) {};\n\\node[dot,label=right:$h_{-J}$] (d) at (9.1,-1.1) {};\n\\node[dot,label=below:$z$] (z) at (0,-2.65) {};\n\\node[dot,label=below:$z'$] (zz) at (8,-2.65) {};\n\\draw[raw] (u)--node[above left] {$J$} (a);\n\\draw[raw] (a) to[out=-10,in=170] (2.6,1.1)\n  to[out=-10,in=180] (4,1.3) to[out=0,in=190] (b);\n\\node[align=center] at (4,2.65) {middle walk of length $L$\\\\\nconditioned to end in the type of $g_J$};\n\\draw[raw] (b)--node[above right] {$J$} (v);\n\\draw[escape] (u)--node[left] {$J$} (c);\n\\draw[escape] (v)--node[right] {$J$} (d);\n\\draw[escape] (c) to[out=-80,in=180] (-.3,-1.9) to[out=0,in=180] (.5,-2.1)\n  to[out=0,in=0] (z);\n\\draw[escape] (d) to[out=-100,in=0] (8.3,-1.9) to[out=180,in=0] (7.5,-2.1)\n  to[out=180,in=180] (zz);\n\\node[align=center,text width=5cm] at (4,-1.2)\n{Walk tails\\\\of length $m=j-J$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A path configuration with a blue raw path at top and orange escape paths below: starting from $u = g_0$, a blue segment labelled $J$ goes up-right to $g_J$, connects via a horizontal dotted segment to $h_J$ labelled \"middle walk of length $L$ conditioned to end in the type of $g_J$\", and a blue segment labelled $J$ goes down-right to $v = h_0$. From $u = g_0$, an orange segment labelled $J$ goes down-left to $g_{-J}$ with an orange walk tail extending to $z$. From $v = h_0$, an orange segment labelled $J$ goes down-left to $h_{-J}$ with an orange walk tail extending to $z'$. Text between tails reads: \"Walk tails of length $m = j - J$\"."
```

```json
{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "Establishing the absence of an infinite critical cluster for nonamenable quasi-transitive graphs is a necessary foundation that settles the exponential growth regime prior to the subexponential analysis.",
      "relation": "prerequisite",
      "source_anchor": "thm:main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-214.1",
      "source_quote": "Let $G$ be an infinite connected locally finite quasi-transitive graph with $h_V(G)>0$.",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-thm-exponential",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-213.2",
      "target_quote": "If a connected locally finite quasi-transitive graph satisfies $\\liminf_{r\\to\\infty}|B(x,r)|^{1/r}>1$, then it has almost surely no infinite Bernoulli bond cluster at its critical probability.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The critical percolation finiteness result on quasi transitive graphs provides the essential absence of infinite clusters at criticality needed for establishing operator bounds.",
      "relation": "uses",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-pre-critical-finiteness",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-214.1",
      "source_quote": "all open clusters are finite almost surely",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-213.2",
      "target_quote": "there is an infinite open cluster",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The general theorem establishes the operator gap and simultaneous nonuniqueness interval for all nonamenable groups which the target paper applies to a concrete group.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-214.1",
      "source_quote": "infinitely many infinite open clusters simultaneously for every",
      "target_anchor": "cor:percolation",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-248.1",
      "target_quote": "infinitely many infinite open clusters simultaneously for every",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    }
```
